In cutting stock (CSP) one assumes $\sum_j d_j\,w_j>c$ ($d_j$ required copies of the piece of length $w_j$, rolls of length $c$). With $c=10$, $w=(3,4)$ and $d=(3,1)$ one has $\sum_j w_j=7\le c$. Is the instance solved with one roll?
  1. No: the assumption fails and the instance is infeasible
  2. No: the copies weigh $13>10$, at least $2$ rolls are needed
  3. Yes: $\sum_j w_j=7\le10$, so everything fits in one roll
  4. Yes: the assumption fails, so the optimum is a single roll
Explanation. The assumption counts all required copies, not one piece per type: $13>10$, so one roll is not enough, and $\lceil13/10\rceil=2$.
oc-f-407 · level facile · notes
A cutting stock (CSP) instance requires $7$ copies in total; the partitions of the $7$ copies, counted as different items, are $B(7)=877$. Why does this number overestimate the truly different cutting plans?
  1. Swapping two identical copies of the same piece gives the same plan
  2. Many partitions have empty bins, which are not counted
  3. The Bell number also counts the orderings of the bins
  4. It does not: every partition is a different plan
Explanation. The copies of the same piece are indistinguishable: partitions differing only in which copy goes into which roll describe the same cut.
oc-f-408 · level facile · notes
Johnson and coauthors (1974) proved that First Fit (every item, in the given order, goes into the first bin where it fits) uses at most $\frac{17}{10}z(I)+2$ bins, where $z(I)$ is the optimum. If $z(I)=20$, how many bins can First Fit use at most?
  1. $34$
  2. $40$
  3. $22$
  4. $36$
Explanation. $\frac{17}{10}\cdot20+2=34+2=36$. $34$ forgets the additive term $+2$; $40$ is ratio $2$.
oc-f-409 · level teorema · notes
For First Fit in bin packing $FF(I)\le\frac{17}{10}z(I)+2$ holds for every instance $I$ ($z(I)$ = optimal number of bins). What does this guarantee say about the ratio $FF(I)/z(I)$?
  1. It is exactly $1.7$ on every instance
  2. It is at least $1.7$: First Fit is never optimal
  3. It is at most $1.7+2/z(I)$: close to $1.7$ when $z(I)$ is large
  4. It is at most $1.7$ for every instance, even with $z(I)=1$
Explanation. Dividing by $z(I)$: $FF/z\le1.7+2/z$. For small instances the term $2/z$ matters; the guarantee is an upper bound, and First Fit may also find the optimum.
oc-f-410 · level facile · notes
Garey, Graham, Ullman and Johnson analysed «in the worst case» simple packing rules such as First Fit. What does a worst-case analysis provide?
  1. The result of the rule on the largest known instance
  2. A guarantee valid on every instance, even the most unfavourable
  3. The average number of bins on random instances
  4. The maximum running time of the rule over all inputs
Explanation. The worst case bounds the number of bins of the rule with respect to the optimum over all instances; the average behaviour is a different analysis.

4. Coloring problems (26)

oc-f-013 · level teorema · notes
For a graph $G=(V,E)$ with $n$ vertices let $\omega(G)$ be the cardinality of a maximum clique, $\alpha(G)$ that of a maximum stable set and $\chi(G)$ the chromatic number (minimum number of colors of a vertex coloring). Which pair of inequalities holds for every graph?
  1. $\chi(G)\le\omega(G)$ and $\chi(G)\le\left\lceil n/\alpha(G)\right\rceil$
  2. $\omega(G)\le\chi(G)$ and $\chi(G)\le\left\lceil n/\alpha(G)\right\rceil$
  3. $\alpha(G)\le\chi(G)$ and $\omega(G)\le\chi(G)$
  4. $\omega(G)\le\chi(G)$ and $\left\lceil n/\alpha(G)\right\rceil\le\chi(G)$
Explanation. A clique needs pairwise different colors (clique bound); every color class is a stable set, with at most $\alpha(G)$ vertices, hence at least $\lceil n/\alpha(G)\rceil$ colors are needed. Both are dual bounds.
oc-f-014 · level ipotesi · notes
For a graph $G$ with $n$ vertices, let $\chi(G)$ be the chromatic number and $\alpha(G)$ the cardinality of a maximum stable set. Which facts are needed to prove the bound $\left\lceil n/\alpha(G)\right\rceil\le\chi(G)$?
  1. The graph contains a clique with $\chi(G)$ vertices; every stable set has at most $\alpha(G)$ vertices; $\chi(G)$ is an integer
  2. Every vertex has degree at most $\Delta(G)$ (maximum degree); there are at most $\Delta(G)+1$ color classes; $\chi(G)$ is an integer
  3. Every color class is a stable set, hence it has at most $\alpha(G)$ vertices; the classes cover the $n$ vertices; $\chi(G)$ is an integer
  4. Every color class is a clique, hence it has at most $\omega(G)$ vertices; the classes cover the $n$ vertices; $\chi(G)$ is an integer
Explanation. With $\chi(G)$ stable classes, each with at most $\alpha(G)$ vertices, we have $\chi(G)\,\alpha(G)\ge n$; since $\chi(G)$ is an integer, we round up.
oc-f-015 · level facile · notes
The Petersen graph has $n=10$ vertices, maximum cliques of $\omega(G)=2$ vertices and maximum stable sets of $\alpha(G)=4$ vertices. What do the clique bound $\omega(G)$ and the stable set bound $\lceil n/\alpha(G)\rceil$ give on the chromatic number $\chi(G)$?
  1. Clique bound $2$, stable set bound $5$: hence $\chi(G)\ge 5$
  2. Clique bound $2$, stable set bound $3$: hence $\chi(G)\ge 3$
  3. Clique bound $2$, stable set bound $2$: hence $\chi(G)\ge 2$
  4. Clique bound $4$, stable set bound $3$: hence $\chi(G)\ge 4$
Explanation. $\omega(G)=2$ and $\lceil 10/4\rceil=3$. The clique bound leaves a gap; the stable set bound closes it, because a coloring with $3$ colors exists.
oc-f-016 · level facile · notes
In the vertex coloring problem (VCP) on a graph $G$ every vertex gets a color, adjacent vertices get different colors, and the number of colors is minimum. A solution can be seen as:
  1. a partition of the vertices into stable sets, up to renaming the colors
  2. a partition of the vertices into cliques, up to renaming the colors
  3. a partition of the edges into matchings, up to renaming the colors
  4. a maximum stable set, plus a different color for every other vertex
Explanation. Every color class is a stable set (non-adjacent vertices can share a color); permuting the colors does not give different solutions.
oc-f-100 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP) we minimize the number of colors, with different colors on adjacent vertices; $\chi(G)$ is the chromatic number and $\omega(G)$ the size of a maximum clique. For the cycle $C_5$ (5 vertices on a circle), what are $\omega(C_5)$, the bound $\lceil n/\alpha\rceil$ (with $\alpha=2$ the maximum stable set) and $\chi(C_5)$?
  1. $2$, $3$ and $3$
  2. $2$, $3$ and $2$
  3. $3$, $3$ and $3$
  4. $2$, $2$ and $3$
Explanation. $C_5$ has no triangle ($\omega=2$), has $\alpha=2$ and hence $\lceil 5/2\rceil=3$; an odd cycle cannot be colored with $2$ colors, and $3$ suffice: $\chi(C_5)=3$.
oc-f-101 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP), a solution is a partition of the vertices into stable sets, up to renaming the colors. How many partitions does a set of $4$ vertices have, i.e., candidate solutions before checking the edges (Bell number $B(4)$)?
  1. $24$
  2. $256$
  3. $16$
  4. $15$
Explanation. $B(4)=15$: one part ($1$), two parts ($7$), three parts ($6$), four parts ($1$). $4^4=256$ counts the assignments of $4$ named colors, $4!=24$ the orderings, $2^4$ the subsets.
oc-f-102 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP), for a graph $G$ we find by hand a clique with $3$ vertices and a feasible coloring with $4$ colors. What do we know about the chromatic number $\chi(G)$?
  1. $\chi(G)=3$: the clique bound always equals the chromatic number
  2. $4\le\chi(G)$: the coloring gives a lower bound, the clique an upper bound
  3. $3\le\chi(G)\le 4$: the clique gives a dual bound, the coloring a primal bound
  4. $\chi(G)=4$: a coloring found by hand is always optimal on small graphs
Explanation. The vertices of the clique need different colors, hence $\chi(G)\ge 3$; the coloring with $4$ colors proves $\chi(G)\le 4$. Without more information the chromatic number can be $3$ or $4$.
oc-f-103 · level facile · notes
In a simple graph $G=(V,E)$, the degree of a vertex is the number of its neighbours. A graph with $6$ vertices has degrees $(3,3,2,2,2,2)$. How many edges does it have?
  1. $15$
  2. $7$
  3. $14$
  4. $6$
Explanation. Every edge has two endpoints, so the sum of the degrees is $2m$: $14=2m$ and $m=7$. The value $15=\binom62$ is the number of edges of the complete graph.
oc-f-104 · level facile · notes
For a graph $G=(V,E)$ and a subset $S\subseteq V$, what is the induced subgraph $G[S]$?
  1. The graph with vertices $S$ and all the edges of $G$ with both endpoints in $S$
  2. The graph with vertices $S$ and all the edges of $G$ with at least one endpoint in $S$
  3. The graph with vertices $V\setminus S$ and all the edges of $G$ among these vertices
  4. The graph with vertices $S$ and only the edges of a spanning tree of $G$
Explanation. $G[S]$ keeps the vertices of $S$ and the edges of $G$ between two vertices of $S$. For example $S$ is a clique if and only if $G[S]$ is complete, and it is stable if and only if $G[S]$ has no edge.
oc-f-105 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP), every color class is a stable set, so it has at most $\alpha(G)$ vertices. A graph has $n=9$ vertices and $\alpha(G)=2$. Which lower bound on the chromatic number $\chi(G)$ follows?
  1. $4$
  2. $7$
  3. $2$
  4. $5$
Explanation. With classes of at most $2$ vertices at least $\lceil 9/2\rceil=5$ classes are needed. The value $4$ forgets to round up, $2$ mistakes $\alpha$ for the bound.
oc-f-106 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP), the wheel $W_5$ has a hub adjacent to all the $5$ vertices of a cycle $C_5$. What are the size $\omega(W_5)$ of a maximum clique and the chromatic number $\chi(W_5)$?
  1. $\omega=4$ and $\chi=4$
  2. $\omega=2$ and $\chi=3$
  3. $\omega=3$ and $\chi=4$
  4. $\omega=3$ and $\chi=3$
Explanation. The hub and two consecutive vertices of the cycle form a triangle, and there is no clique with $4$ vertices: $\omega=3$. The odd cycle needs $3$ colors and the hub a fourth one: $\chi=4>\omega$.
oc-f-107 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP) of the complete graph $K_6$ ($6$ pairwise adjacent vertices), what is the chromatic number, and which bound certifies it?
  1. $5$, because the largest degree is $\Delta=5$
  2. $6$, certified by the clique bound $\omega(K_6)=6$
  3. $2$, because every graph with an edge has $\chi\ge 2$
  4. $3$, certified by the stable set bound $\lceil 6/2\rceil$
Explanation. All the vertices are adjacent, so $6$ different colors are needed: $\omega(K_6)=6\le\chi(K_6)\le 6$. The largest degree $5$ gives only the upper bound $\Delta+1=6$.
oc-f-261 · level facile · notes
In the vertex coloring problem (VCP) of a graph $G=(V,E)$ the two endpoints of every edge get different colors. What do the vertices with the same color form, and hence what does the VCP look for?
  1. A clique; the VCP looks for a partition of $V$ into the minimum number of cliques
  2. A stable set; the VCP looks for the largest stable set
  3. A path; the VCP looks for the longest path
  4. A stable set; the VCP looks for a partition of $V$ into the minimum number of stable sets
Explanation. Two vertices of the same color cannot be adjacent: every color class is a stable set. The minimum number of classes is the chromatic number $\chi(G)$.
oc-f-262 · level facile · notes
In a graph $G$ a clique is a set of pairwise adjacent vertices and a stable set a set of pairwise non-adjacent vertices. What is a stable set of $G$ in the complement graph $\overline G$ (same vertices, adjacent if and only if they are not in $G$)?
  1. A cycle of $\overline G$
  2. A set of isolated vertices of $\overline G$
  3. A clique of $\overline G$
  4. A stable set of $\overline G$
Explanation. Two non-adjacent vertices in $G$ are adjacent in $\overline G$: hence $\alpha(G)=\omega(\overline G)$. A single vertex is both a clique and a stable set.
oc-f-263 · level ipotesi · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP) one assumes that no vertex has its neighbourhood contained in that of another vertex. If instead $N(u)\subseteq N(v)$ with $u\ne v$, why can $u$ be removed without changing the chromatic number?
  1. Removing $u$ always decreases the chromatic number by one
  2. $u$ and $v$ are not adjacent, and every coloring of the rest of the graph extends by giving $u$ the color of $v$
  3. $u$ has smaller degree, so it always gets color $1$
  4. $u$ and $v$ are adjacent, so $u$ gets a new color
Explanation. If they were adjacent, $v\in N(u)\subseteq N(v)$, impossible in a simple graph. The neighbours of $u$ are neighbours of $v$, so none of them has the color of $v$: $u$ can take it.
oc-f-264 · level ipotesi · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP) one assumes that the graph has no isolated vertices ($\deg(v)\ge1$ for every $v$). Why is the assumption not restrictive?
  1. An isolated vertex can get any color already used, so it does not change the chromatic number (if the graph has at least one edge)
  2. An isolated vertex always requires a new color
  3. A graph with isolated vertices cannot be colored
  4. The isolated vertices always form a clique
Explanation. A vertex without neighbours has no conflicts: it is colored with color $1$. If the graph has no edges the optimal value is $1$.
oc-f-265 · level facile · notes
The Petersen graph has $n=10$ vertices, no triangle ($\omega(G)=2$) and stability number $\alpha(G)=4$; a coloring with $3$ colors is known. Using the clique bound $\omega(G)$ and the stable set bound $\lceil n/\alpha(G)\rceil$, what can be concluded?
  1. The clique bound gives $3$, hence $\chi(G)=3$
  2. The bounds give $2$, so a gap remains and $\chi(G)$ is unknown
  3. $\chi(G)=4$, because $\alpha(G)=4$
  4. The stable set bound gives $3$, which matches the coloring: $\chi(G)=3$
Explanation. $\omega(G)=2$ leaves a gap of $1$; $\lceil10/4\rceil=3$ closes the gap with the $3$-coloring.
oc-f-266 · level facile · notes
In vertex coloring (VCP) the candidate solutions are the partitions of $V$, counted by the Bell numbers $B(n)$. For a triangle ($n=3$, all vertices adjacent), how many candidates are there and how many are feasible?
  1. $5$ candidates, $3$ feasible
  2. $6$ candidates, $6$ feasible
  3. $5$ candidates, $1$ feasible (three singletons)
  4. $8$ candidates, $1$ feasible
Explanation. $B(3)=5$; every class with two vertices contains an edge, so only the partition into three singletons is feasible and $\chi=3$.
oc-f-267 · level ipotesi · notes
Brooks' theorem (1941): a connected graph that is neither complete nor an odd cycle can be colored with at most $\Delta$ colors ($\Delta$ = maximum degree). What does the cycle on $5$ vertices $C_5$ show?
  1. That $C_5$ needs $\Delta+2=4$ colors
  2. That the assumption «not an odd cycle» is needed: $\Delta=2$ but $\chi(C_5)=3$
  3. That the theorem also holds for odd cycles: $\chi(C_5)=2$
  4. That the connectivity assumption is needed: $C_5$ is not connected
Explanation. An odd cycle cannot be colored with $2$ colors, so it exceeds $\Delta=2$ by one; the complete graph $K_n$ also has $\chi=n=\Delta+1$.
oc-f-411 · level facile · notes
Some exams must be timetabled: two exams with common students cannot be in the same time slot, and the minimum number of slots is wanted. How is this modelled as vertex colouring?
  1. Vertices = exams, edges = pairs without common students, colours = slots
  2. Vertices = exams, edges = pairs with common students, colours = slots
  3. Vertices = slots, edges = exams, colours = students
  4. Vertices = students, edges = common exams, colours = slots
Explanation. The edges join the conflicting pairs; a colour class is a stable set, i.e., a group of exams without conflicts, and the minimum number of slots is $\chi(G)$.
oc-f-412 · level teorema · notes
In vertex colouring of $G$ the vertices of the same colour form a stable set. Let $\overline{G}$ be the complement (two vertices are adjacent in $\overline{G}$ if and only if they are not in $G$). What do those vertices form in $\overline{G}$, and what is colouring $G$ equivalent to?
  1. A clique; covering $\overline{G}$ with the minimum number of cliques
  2. A stable set; colouring $\overline{G}$ with the same colours
  3. A clique; finding a maximum clique of $\overline{G}$
  4. A cycle; finding a tour through the vertices of $\overline{G}$
Explanation. Vertices pairwise non-adjacent in $G$ are pairwise adjacent in $\overline{G}$. A colouring of $G$ is therefore a partition of the vertices of $\overline{G}$ into cliques.
oc-f-413 · level teorema · notes
In vertex colouring, if $N(u)\subseteq N(v)$ ($N(\cdot)$ = set of neighbours) for two distinct vertices, $u$ is removed and later receives the colour of $v$. Why are $u$ and $v$ non-adjacent, so that they can have the same colour?
  1. Because $N(u)\subseteq N(v)$ always implies $\deg(u)=\deg(v)$
  2. Because a graph without isolated vertices has no edges between nested neighbourhoods
  3. It is not true in general: it must be checked case by case
  4. Otherwise $v\in N(u)\subseteq N(v)$: $v$ would be adjacent to itself
Explanation. A simple graph has no loops, so $v\notin N(v)$. From $\{u,v\}\in E$ it would follow $v\in N(u)\subseteq N(v)$, a contradiction.
oc-f-414 · level facile · notes
The Petersen graph has $10$ vertices: $1,\dots,5$ form a cycle of $5$ vertices, and so do $6,\dots,10$ (plus other edges between the two cycles). Why does a stable set (pairwise non-adjacent vertices) have at most $4$ vertices?
  1. Every vertex has degree $3$, so a stable set has at most $10-3-3$ vertices
  2. The graph is $3$-colourable, so $\alpha\le\lceil10/3\rceil=4$
  3. A stable set takes at most $2$ vertices from each of the two cycles
  4. The graph has no triangles, so stable sets have at most $4$ vertices
Explanation. In a $5$-vertex cycle any three vertices include two adjacent ones. The absence of triangles bounds the cliques, not the stable sets; and $\chi=3$ would give $\alpha\ge\lceil10/3\rceil$, not $\le$.
oc-f-415 · level teorema · notes
Brooks' theorem (1941): a connected graph that is neither complete nor an odd cycle can be coloured with at most $\Delta$ colours ($\Delta$ = maximum degree). The Petersen graph is connected, all its vertices have degree $3$, and it is neither complete nor a cycle. What does the theorem give?
  1. $\chi(G)\le2$, because there are no triangles
  2. $\chi(G)\le3$, because $\Delta=3$
  3. $\chi(G)\le4$: the theorem does not apply to regular graphs
  4. $\chi(G)=3$: the theorem also gives the lower bound
Explanation. The theorem is an upper bound: $\chi\le\Delta=3$. That $\chi$ is exactly $3$ follows from a different lower bound (e.g., $\lceil n/\alpha\rceil=\lceil10/4\rceil$).
oc-f-416 · level ipotesi · notes
Brooks' theorem ($\chi(G)\le\Delta$ for a connected graph, $\Delta$ = maximum degree) excludes complete graphs and odd cycles. Why are these exceptions necessary?
  1. In both $\chi=\Delta+1$, so $\Delta$ colours are not enough
  2. In both $\chi<\Delta$, so the bound would be too weak
  3. They are the only graphs in which the maximum degree is undefined
  4. They are disconnected graphs, to which the theorem does not apply
Explanation. $K_n$ has $\Delta=n-1$ and $\chi=n$, an odd cycle $\Delta=2$ and $\chi=3$. Without the exceptions the theorem would be false: e.g., $K_4$ has maximum degree $3$ but needs $4$ colours; $C_5$ has degree $2$ but needs $3$ colours.
oc-f-417 · level facile · notes
Garey, Johnson and Stockmeyer (1976) proved that deciding whether $3$ colours suffice to colour a graph remains NP-complete even on planar graphs of maximum degree $4$. What follows, if $P\neq NP$?
  1. On planar graphs of degree $4$ one always has $\chi(G)\le3$
  2. On planar graphs colouring is solved in polynomial time
  3. Deciding whether $2$ colours suffice is NP-complete as well
  4. Not even there does a polynomial algorithm decide if $\chi(G)\le3$
Explanation. The difficulty remains even when the class of graphs is strongly restricted. Deciding whether $2$ colours suffice (bipartite graph) is instead easy.

5. Clique problems (32)

oc-f-017 · level teorema · notes
Let $\overline{G}$ be the complement of the graph $G$ (same vertices; two vertices are adjacent in $\overline{G}$ if and only if they are not adjacent in $G$), and let $\omega$ and $\alpha$ be the cardinalities of a maximum clique and of a maximum stable set. Which statement holds for every graph $G$ with $n$ vertices?
  1. $K$ is stable in $G$ if and only if $V\setminus K$ is a clique of $G$; hence $\omega(G)+\alpha(G)=n$
  2. $K$ is a clique of $G$ if and only if it is a stable set of $\overline{G}$; hence $\omega(G)=\alpha(\overline{G})$
  3. $K$ is a clique of $G$ if and only if it is a stable set of $G$; hence $\omega(G)=\alpha(G)$
  4. $K$ is a clique of $G$ if and only if it is a clique of $\overline{G}$; hence $\omega(G)=\omega(\overline{G})$
Explanation. The edges of $\overline{G}$ are the non-adjacent pairs of $G$: pairwise adjacent vertices in $G$ are pairwise non-adjacent in $\overline{G}$. Hence the MCP on $G$ is the MSSP on $\overline{G}$.
oc-f-018 · level facile · notes
A graph $G$ has $m=10$ edges. A clique of $k$ vertices contains $k(k-1)/2$ edges, hence the cardinality $\omega(G)$ of a maximum clique satisfies $\omega(G)(\omega(G)-1)/2\le m$. What is the smallest upper bound on $\omega(G)$ that follows?
  1. $5$
  2. $4$
  3. $6$
  4. $10$
Explanation. With $\omega=5$ we get $5\cdot 4/2=10\le 10$; with $\omega=6$ we would get $15>10$. Hence $\omega(G)\le 5$.
oc-f-019 · level facile · notes
A graph $G$ has $n=7$ vertices and $m=12$ edges. The complement $\overline{G}$ has the same vertices, and two vertices are adjacent in $\overline{G}$ if and only if they are not adjacent in $G$. How many edges does $\overline{G}$ have?
  1. $12$
  2. $30$
  3. $37$
  4. $9$
Explanation. $|\overline{E}|=\binom{n}{2}-m=21-12=9$: the edges of $\overline{G}$ are the pairs that are not edges of $G$.
oc-f-020 · level facile · notes
In a graph, a clique is a set of pairwise adjacent vertices. What is the difference between a maximal clique and a maximum clique?
  1. It is maximal if it contains a maximum clique; it is maximum if no vertex can be added to it
  2. They are synonyms: no vertex can be added to it and no clique has more vertices
  3. It is maximal if no vertex can be added to it; it is maximum if no clique has more vertices
  4. It is maximum if no vertex can be added to it; it is maximal if no clique has more vertices
Explanation. Every maximum clique is maximal, but not conversely: a maximal clique can be smaller than $\omega(G)$.
oc-f-092 · level teorema · notes
In the maximum clique problem (MCP) on a graph $G$, let $\Delta$ be the largest degree of a vertex and $\omega(G)$ the size of a maximum clique. Which bound holds for every graph, and why?
  1. $\omega(G)\ge\Delta+1$: a vertex of degree $\Delta$ and its neighbours form a clique
  2. $\omega(G)\le\Delta-1$: in a clique with $k$ vertices every vertex has at most $k-2$ neighbours
  3. $\omega(G)\le\Delta+1$: in a clique with $k$ vertices every vertex has at least $k-1$ neighbours
  4. $\omega(G)\le\Delta$: in a clique with $k$ vertices every vertex has at least $k$ neighbours
Explanation. A vertex of a clique $K$ is adjacent to the other $|K|-1$ vertices of $K$, hence $|K|-1\le\Delta$. The neighbours of a vertex are not pairwise adjacent in general, so they do not form a clique.
oc-f-093 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem (MCP), $k^{\max}$ is the largest integer $k$ such that at least $k$ vertices have degree at least $k-1$, and $\omega(G)\le k^{\max}$. A graph has degrees $(4,3,3,2,2,1)$. What is $k^{\max}$?
  1. $2$
  2. $3$
  3. $5$
  4. $4$
Explanation. For $k=4$ we need $4$ vertices of degree at least $3$, but there are only $3$; for $k=3$ we need $3$ vertices of degree at least $2$, and there are $5$. Hence $k^{\max}=3$; the degree bound $\Delta+1$ would give $5$.
oc-f-094 · level teorema · notes
In the maximum clique problem (MCP), let $\Delta$ be the largest degree and $k^{\max}$ the largest $k$ such that at least $k$ vertices have degree at least $k-1$. How are the two upper bounds $k^{\max}$ and $\Delta+1$ on $\omega(G)$ related?
  1. $k^{\max}\le\Delta+1$ for every graph: the degree count bound is never weaker
  2. $k^{\max}\ge\Delta+1$ for every graph: the degree bound is never weaker
  3. $k^{\max}=\Delta+1$ for every graph: the two bounds always coincide
  4. They are not comparable: on some graphs one wins, on others the other
Explanation. If $k$ vertices have degree at least $k-1$, then $\Delta\ge k-1$; hence $k^{\max}\le\Delta+1$. For example with degrees $(4,3,3,2,2,1)$ we get $k^{\max}=3<5=\Delta+1$.
oc-f-095 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem (MCP) on a graph with $n=10$ vertices, a candidate solution is any subset of the vertices, to be checked pair by pair afterwards. How many candidate solutions are there?
  1. $45$
  2. $3\,628\,800$
  3. $512$
  4. $1024$
Explanation. The subsets of $10$ vertices are $2^{10}=1024$. $\binom{10}{2}=45$ counts the pairs of vertices, $10!$ the orderings; $2^9$ forgets half of the subsets.
oc-f-096 · level facile · notes
The decision version of the maximum clique problem (MCP) asks whether a graph with $n$ vertices has a clique with $k$ vertices. What can be said if $k$ is a fixed constant (for example $k=3$: is there a triangle)?
  1. It is decided in $O(2^k n)$ steps, polynomial even when $k$ is part of the input
  2. It is decided in $O(n^2)$ steps for every $k$, checking all the pairs only once
  3. It is decided in $O(n^k k^2)$ steps, polynomial for fixed $k$; with $k$ part of the input it stays NP-complete
  4. It stays NP-complete even for $k=3$, because the number of subsets is $2^n$
Explanation. The subsets with $k$ vertices are $\binom nk\le n^k$, and each is checked in $O(k^2)$: polynomial for fixed $k$, but the exponent grows with $k$. With $k$ given in the input the problem is one of Karp's 21 NP-complete problems.
oc-f-097 · level facile · notes
In the maximum clique problem (MCP), the vertices of a graph $G$ are partitioned into $4$ stable sets (sets of pairwise non-adjacent vertices). What follows for the size $\omega(G)$ of a maximum clique?
  1. Nothing: stable sets give bounds only on the maximum stable set
  2. $\omega(G)\le 4$, because a clique contains at most one vertex of each stable set
  3. $\omega(G)\ge 4$, because a clique contains at least one vertex of each stable set
  4. $\omega(G)=4$, because the classes of a partition into stable sets are cliques
Explanation. Two vertices of the same stable set are not adjacent, so a clique takes at most one of them: $\omega(G)\le 4$. It is the coloring bound $\omega(G)\le\chi(G)$, since the partition is a coloring with $4$ colors.
oc-f-098 · level facile · notes
In the weighted variants of the maximum clique, every vertex $v$ has a weight $w_v$ and every edge $\{u,v\}$ a weight $r_{uv}$; $w(K)$ and $r(K)$ add up the weights of the vertices and of the edges of the clique $K$. For the triangle $K=\{1,2,3\}$ with $w=(3,5,2)$, $r_{12}=4$, $r_{13}=1$, $r_{23}=6$, what are $w(K)$ and $r(K)$?
  1. $w(K)=10$ and $r(K)=11$
  2. $w(K)=11$ and $r(K)=10$
  3. $w(K)=10$ and $r(K)=21$
  4. $w(K)=10$ and $r(K)=6$
Explanation. $w(K)=3+5+2=10$ and $r(K)=4+1+6=11$. The value $21$ adds vertices and edges together, $6$ takes only the heaviest edge.
oc-f-099 · level facile · notes
In the edge-weighted maximum clique problem (EWMCP) we look for a clique $K$ maximizing $r(K)$, the sum of the weights $r_{uv}$ of its edges. Why, with all the weights $r_{uv}=1$, is the EWMCP the maximum clique problem (MCP)?
  1. Because $r(K)=|K|$, the number of vertices of the clique
  2. Because $r(K)=2|K|$, since every vertex has two edges
  3. Because $r(K)=1$ for every clique, and all cliques are optimal
  4. Because $r(K)=\binom{|K|}{2}$, which grows with the number $|K|$ of vertices
Explanation. A clique with $k$ vertices has $\binom k2$ edges, and $\binom k2$ is increasing in $k$: maximizing $r(K)$ is the same as maximizing $|K|$.
oc-f-268 · level facile · notes
The family of clique problems includes the vertex-weighted maximum clique (VWMCP) and the edge-weighted one (EWMCP). What does the EWMCP maximize?
  1. The number of vertices of the clique
  2. The weight of the heaviest edge of the clique
  3. The total weight of the edges with both endpoints in the clique
  4. The total weight of the vertices of the clique
Explanation. In the EWMCP a clique with $k$ vertices counts the weights of its $\binom k2$ edges; in the VWMCP the vertex weights are summed.
oc-f-269 · level facile · notes
The clique number $\omega(G)$ is the largest number of pairwise adjacent vertices. What is obtained by solving the maximum clique problem on the complement graph $\overline G$?
  1. The maximum degree of $G$
  2. A maximum stable set of $G$, of cardinality $\alpha(G)$
  3. A maximum clique of $G$
  4. An optimal coloring of $G$
Explanation. The cliques of $\overline G$ are the stable sets of $G$: $\omega(\overline G)=\alpha(G)$. This is why the MSSP is as hard as the MCP.
oc-f-270 · level ipotesi · notes
For the maximum clique problem one considers instances with $1\le m<\binom n2$ ($m$ edges, $n$ vertices). Why do the two excluded cases not lose generality?
  1. With $m=0$ every vertex is a maximum clique ($\omega=1$); with $m=\binom n2$ the graph is complete ($\omega=n$)
  2. With $m=0$ the problem is infeasible; with $m=\binom n2$ there are no cliques
  3. With $m=0$ one has $\omega=0$; with $m=\binom n2$ one has $\omega=n-1$
  4. In both cases $\omega=2$
Explanation. The two extreme cases are solved by inspection: without edges any vertex, with the complete graph the whole $V$.
oc-f-271 · level facile · notes
A graph has $8$ vertices split into the pairs $\{1,5\},\{2,6\},\{3,7\},\{4,8\}$: the two vertices of a pair are not adjacent, all other pairs of vertices are. What is $\omega(G)$ and how many maximum cliques are there?
  1. $\omega(G)=4$, with a single maximum clique
  2. $\omega(G)=8$, with a single maximum clique
  3. $\omega(G)=2$, with $4$ maximum cliques
  4. $\omega(G)=4$, with $16$ maximum cliques
Explanation. A clique takes at most one vertex per pair; taking exactly one per pair gives a clique: $2^4=16$ cliques of $4$ vertices.
oc-f-272 · level facile · notes
To build a clique by hand one starts from a vertex of maximum degree and adds, one at a time, a vertex adjacent to all those already chosen, while possible. What does the resulting clique $K$ provide?
  1. Always a maximum clique
  2. The chromatic number $\chi(G)$
  3. A primal bound: $|K|\le\omega(G)$
  4. A dual bound: $|K|\ge\omega(G)$
Explanation. $K$ is a clique, hence feasible: in a maximization problem its value is a lower bound. It need not be maximum, because the choices are made one at a time.
oc-f-273 · level teorema · notes
For a fixed integer $k$, all $\binom nk$ subsets of $k$ vertices can be checked in $O(n^k k^2)$ steps, a polynomial in $n$. Why does this not contradict the NP-hardness of the maximum clique problem?
  1. It does contradict it: the problem is in P
  2. The exponent grows with $k$, and $k$ is part of the input: for $k$ of the order of $n$ the time is not polynomial
  3. Because $O(n^k k^2)$ is exponential even for fixed $k$
  4. Because checking a clique requires exponential time
Explanation. A polynomial algorithm must have a fixed exponent for all instances; here the exponent depends on $k$, which can grow with $n$.
oc-f-274 · level facile · notes
Håstad (1999) proved an inapproximability result for maximum clique ($n$ vertices, $\omega(G)$ clique number). Which one, unless NP is contained in randomized polynomial time?
  1. For every $\varepsilon>0$ no polynomial algorithm guarantees a clique of at least $\omega(G)/n^{1-\varepsilon}$ vertices
  2. No polynomial algorithm guarantees a clique of at least $\omega(G)/2$ vertices, but $\omega(G)/3$ is possible
  3. There is an FPTAS for maximum clique
  4. Every greedy algorithm guarantees $\omega(G)-1$ vertices
Explanation. A single-vertex clique already guarantees $\omega(G)/n$: the result says that one cannot do substantially better than this trivial ratio.
oc-f-275 · level facile · notes
In a vertex coloring of a graph $G$ every color class is a stable set. What is the link with the maximum stable set problem (MSSP)?
  1. The MSSP looks for the minimum number of colors
  2. The MSSP looks for the largest clique, which is a color class
  3. No link: the color classes are cliques
  4. The MSSP looks for the largest possible color class, of cardinality $\alpha(G)$
Explanation. Every stable set can be a color class; the largest one has $\alpha(G)$ vertices, which gives the bound $\chi(G)\ge\lceil n/\alpha(G)\rceil$.
oc-f-276 · level facile · notes
In the design of error-correcting codes one looks for a large set of pairwise «far» words (distance at least $d$). On which graph is this a maximum clique problem?
  1. On the complete graph of the words
  2. On the graph with an edge between words of different length
  3. On the graph with one word per vertex and an edge between two words at distance at least $d$
  4. On the graph with an edge between two words at distance less than $d$
Explanation. Pairwise compatible words must be pairwise adjacent: a clique. On the graph with the «close» edges it would be a stable set instead.
oc-f-418 · level facile · notes
A clique of a graph $G=(V,E)$ is a set of pairwise adjacent vertices. With $V=\{1,2,3,4\}$ and $E=\{\{1,2\},\{1,3\},\{2,3\},\{3,4\}\}$, which set is a clique?
  1. $\{1,2,3\}$
  2. $\{2,3,4\}$
  3. $\{1,3,4\}$
  4. $\{1,2,3,4\}$
Explanation. $\{1,2,3\}$ has the edges $12,13,23$. In $\{2,3,4\}$ the edge $\{2,4\}$ is missing, in $\{1,3,4\}$ the edge $\{1,4\}$; hence $\omega(G)=3$.
oc-f-419 · level facile · notes
A clique $K$ of a graph $G$ induces a complete graph. If $|K|=5$, how many edges of $G$ have both endpoints in $K$?
  1. $5$
  2. $20$
  3. $25$
  4. $10$
Explanation. All pairs of vertices of $K$ are edges: $\binom52=10$. $20=5\cdot4$ counts every edge twice.
oc-f-420 · level ipotesi · notes
For the maximum clique problem one considers graphs with $1\le m<\binom n2$ ($n$ vertices, $m$ edges). A graph has $n=5$ and $m=10$. Does it satisfy the assumption, and what is $\omega(G)$?
  1. No: the graph has no edges and $\omega(G)=1$
  2. Yes, and $\omega(G)$ must be computed by an algorithm
  3. No: the graph is complete and $\omega(G)=5$
  4. Yes, and $\omega(G)=4$ since an edge is missing
Explanation. $\binom52=10$: all possible edges are present, so $V$ is a clique. It is one of the two trivial cases excluded by the assumption.
oc-f-421 · level teorema · notes
Under the assumption $1\le m<\binom n2$ (at least one edge and at least one pair of non-adjacent vertices), which bounds hold for the clique number $\omega(G)$?
  1. $1\le\omega(G)\le n-1$
  2. $2\le\omega(G)\le n-1$
  3. $1\le\omega(G)\le n$
  4. $2\le\omega(G)\le n$
Explanation. An edge is a clique of $2$ vertices; a non-adjacent pair prevents $V$ from being a clique, so $\omega(G)<n$.
oc-f-422 · level teorema · notes
In the maximum clique problem, why does a graph with $\omega(G)=4$ have at least $2^4=16$ cliques, counting the empty set?
  1. Every subset of a clique is a clique
  2. Every vertex belongs to at least $4$ cliques
  3. The number of cliques is always $2^{\omega(G)}$
  4. Every clique is contained in a maximum clique
Explanation. Pairwise adjacent vertices remain so in every subset: the $2^4$ subsets of a maximum clique are cliques. The number of cliques may be larger.
oc-f-423 · level facile · notes
For the maximum clique on $n$ vertices, brute force lists all vertex subsets and checks whether each one is a clique, in $O(2^n\,n^2)$ steps. Where does the factor $n^2$ come from?
  1. From the number of edges of the complete graph
  2. From sorting the vertices by degree
  3. From computing the complement of the graph
  4. From checking the pairs of vertices of every subset
Explanation. A subset $S$ is a clique if each of its $\binom{|S|}2=O(n^2)$ pairs is an edge; the factor $2^n$ counts the subsets.
oc-f-424 · level teorema · notes
The decision version of maximum clique is NP-complete (Karp, 1972). Why does it follow that the maximum stable set problem (pairwise non-adjacent vertices) is NP-hard too?
  1. Every graph has $\alpha(G)=\omega(G)$, so the two problems coincide
  2. The stable set problem contains colouring as a special case
  3. $\alpha(G)=\omega(\overline{G})$, and $\overline{G}$ is built in polynomial time
  4. Every stable set is also a clique of the same graph
Explanation. A polynomial algorithm for maximum stable set, applied to $\overline{G}$, would give $\omega(G)$ in polynomial time; the reduction is the construction of the complement.
oc-f-425 · level facile · notes
Vertex-weighted maximum clique (VWMCP: the sum $w(K)$ of the vertex weights of the clique $K$ is maximized). A graph has $8$ vertices and all edges except the pairs $\{1,5\},\{2,6\},\{3,7\},\{4,8\}$; the weights are $w=(7,3,5,6,4,8,2,9)$. Optimal value?
  1. $21$
  2. $29$
  3. $30$
  4. $44$
Explanation. A clique takes at most one vertex per pair; the heaviest of each: $7+8+5+9=29$. $30=9+8+7+6$ uses vertices $4$ and $8$, which are not adjacent.
oc-f-426 · level teorema · notes
In the vertex-weighted maximum clique problem (VWMCP) the weights are $w_v\ge1$. Why is an optimal clique always maximal (no outside vertex is adjacent to all its vertices)?
  1. Otherwise that vertex is added and the weight strictly increases
  2. Because an optimal clique always coincides with a maximum clique
  3. Because the weights are integer
  4. It is not true: an optimal clique may be non-maximal
Explanation. With positive weights adding a feasible vertex increases $w(K)$. An optimal clique, however, need not have maximum cardinality.
oc-f-427 · level facile · notes
One wants to hold in parallel the maximum number of pairwise compatible exams (no common students). On which graph is this a maximum clique problem?
  1. On the graph with an edge between every pair of conflicting exams
  2. On the graph with a vertex per student and an edge per exam
  3. On the complete graph on the exams
  4. On the graph with an edge between every pair of compatible exams
Explanation. Pairwise compatible exams are a clique of the compatibility graph, i.e., a stable set of the conflict graph (its complement).
oc-f-428 · level teorema · notes
In a vertex colouring of a graph $G$ with $n$ vertices every colour class is a stable set. Which lower bound on $\alpha(G)$ (cardinality of a maximum stable set) follows, with $\chi(G)$ the chromatic number?
  1. $\alpha(G)\ge n-\chi(G)$
  2. $\alpha(G)\ge\lfloor\chi(G)/2\rfloor$
  3. $\alpha(G)\ge\lceil n/\chi(G)\rceil$
  4. $\alpha(G)\ge\chi(G)$
Explanation. The $\chi(G)$ classes cover the $n$ vertices: the largest has at least $\lceil n/\chi(G)\rceil$ vertices and is a stable set.

6. Routing problems (31)

oc-f-021 · level facile · notes
In the symmetric traveling salesman problem (TSP) ($d_{ij}=d_{ji}$) a tour visits every city once and returns to the start; two tours are equal if they use the same edges. How many distinct tours are there with $n=7$ cities?
  1. $5040$
  2. $2520$
  3. $360$
  4. $720$
Explanation. The tours are $\frac{(n-1)!}{2}=\frac{720}{2}=360$: neither the starting city nor the direction changes the tour. In the asymmetric case they would be $(n-1)!=720$.
oc-f-022 · level facile · notes
In the symmetric TSP with integer distances, let $a_i$ and $b_i$ be the two smallest distances from city $i$ to the others. In an instance with $5$ cities the sums $a_i+b_i$ are $5,7,6,8,9$. Which lower bound is obtained on the length $z(I)$ of the shortest tour?
  1. $17.5$
  2. $18$
  3. $35$
  4. $17$
Explanation. $z(I)\ge\left\lceil\frac12\sum_i(a_i+b_i)\right\rceil=\lceil 35/2\rceil=18$: every edge of the tour is counted by both its endpoints, and the lengths are integer.
oc-f-023 · level ipotesi · notes
In the symmetric TSP let $a_i$ and $b_i$ be the two smallest distances from city $i$ to the others and $z(I)$ the length of the shortest tour. Which facts are needed to prove $z(I)\ge\left\lceil\frac12\sum_{i}(a_i+b_i)\right\rceil$?
  1. In a tour every city has two edges, of length at least $a_i$ and $b_i$; every edge has two endpoints; distances are integer
  2. The triangle inequality, which allows shortcutting the tour; every edge has two endpoints; distances are integer
  3. A minimum spanning tree is shorter than the optimal tour; every edge has two endpoints; distances are integer
  4. In a tour every city has two edges; the nearest neighbour tour is optimal; distances are integer
Explanation. Summing over all the cities the lengths of their two tour edges counts every edge twice: $2z(I)\ge\sum_i(a_i+b_i)$; with integer distances we round up. The triangle inequality is not needed.
oc-f-024 · level facile · notes
In the TSP with distances $d_{ij}\ge 0$ between the cities, when is an instance called metric?
  1. When $d_{ij}\ge d_{ik}+d_{kj}$ for all triples of distinct cities $i,j,k$
  2. When $d_{ij}\le d_{ik}+d_{kj}$ for at least one triple of distinct cities $i,j,k$
  3. When $d_{ij}=d_{ji}$ for all pairs of distinct cities $i,j$
  4. When $d_{ij}\le d_{ik}+d_{kj}$ for all triples of distinct cities $i,j,k$
Explanation. The metric TSP requires the triangle inequality on all triples; holding for one triple is not enough, and symmetry alone is not enough.
oc-f-126 · level teorema · notes
In the asymmetric TSP (ATSP) the distance $d_{ij}$ from $i$ to $j$ may differ from $d_{ji}$, so a tour and its reverse are different solutions. How many distinct tours does an ATSP instance with $n=5$ cities have?
  1. $60$
  2. $24$
  3. $12$
  4. $120$
Explanation. Fixing the starting city leaves $(n-1)!=24$ orderings, and in the asymmetric case the direction matters. In the symmetric case one divides by $2$: $12$.
oc-f-127 · level facile · notes
In the TSP the length of the tour $(\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_n)$ is $\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}d_{\sigma_k\sigma_{k+1}}+d_{\sigma_n\sigma_1}$. With $d_{12}=3$, $d_{23}=4$, $d_{34}=5$, $d_{41}=6$, how long is the tour $(1,2,3,4)$?
  1. $18$
  2. $12$
  3. $15$
  4. $21$
Explanation. $3+4+5+6=18$. The value $12$ forgets the return edge $d_{41}$, which is part of the tour.
oc-f-128 · level facile · notes
Symmetric TSP with $4$ cities: $d_{12}=2$, $d_{13}=5$, $d_{14}=4$, $d_{23}=3$, $d_{24}=6$, $d_{34}=1$. The nearest neighbour rule starts from city $1$, goes each time to the nearest unvisited city and finally returns to $1$. How long is the tour?
  1. $6$
  2. $14$
  3. $12$
  4. $10$
Explanation. $1\to2$ ($2$), $2\to3$ ($3$), $3\to4$ ($1$), return $4\to1$ ($4$): $10$. Without the return one gets $6$; the tour $(1,2,4,3)$ has length $14$.
oc-f-129 · level facile · notes
Symmetric TSP with $4$ cities: $d_{12}=2$, $d_{13}=5$, $d_{14}=4$, $d_{23}=3$, $d_{24}=6$, $d_{34}=1$. With $a_i,b_i$ the two smallest distances from city $i$, the lower bound is $\left\lceil\frac12\sum_i(a_i+b_i)\right\rceil$. What is it?
  1. $9$
  2. $5$
  3. $10$
  4. $20$
Explanation. The sums $a_i+b_i$ are $6,5,4,5$, total $20$, and half of it is $10$. The tour $(1,2,3,4)$ has length $10$: the bound proves it optimal.
oc-f-130 · level teorema · notes
In the TSP, a closed walk through all the cities that repeats some of them is turned into a tour by skipping the cities already visited (shortcuts). Under which assumption on the distances $d_{ij}$ is the resulting tour not longer than the closed walk?
  1. All distances equal, $d_{ij}=d$ for every pair
  2. The triangle inequality $d_{ij}\le d_{ik}+d_{kj}$ for all triples
  3. Symmetry $d_{ij}=d_{ji}$ for all pairs of cities
  4. Positive integer distances, $d_{ij}\in\Z_{\ge 1}$
Explanation. Skipping city $k$ replaces $d_{ik}+d_{kj}$ by $d_{ij}$, which is not larger by the triangle inequality; repeating for every skip the walk does not get longer. It is the key step of the double tree and Christofides algorithms.
oc-f-131 · level facile · notes
A TSP instance is metric if $d_{ij}\le d_{ik}+d_{kj}$ for all triples of distinct cities. On $3$ cities with $d_{12}=3$, $d_{23}=4$, $d_{13}=8$, is the instance metric?
  1. No: $d_{13}=8>d_{12}+d_{23}=7$
  2. Yes: all distances are positive
  3. Yes: $d_{12}+d_{13}\ge d_{23}$
  4. No: $d_{12}<d_{23}$
Explanation. One violating triple is enough: going from $1$ to $3$ through $2$ costs $7<8$. Positive distances are not enough, and the order between $d_{12}$ and $d_{23}$ does not matter.
oc-f-132 · level facile · notes
In the symmetric TSP with $n$ cities the distinct tours are $(n-1)!/2$. Why is listing all of them impossible already with $n=20$ cities?
  1. $2^{20}\approx 10^{6}$ tours: too many to store in a table
  2. $20^2=400$ tours, but each tour needs exponential time for its length
  3. $20!\approx 2\cdot 10^{18}$ tours: the division by $2$ does not apply to the symmetric case
  4. $19!/2\approx 6\cdot 10^{16}$ tours: even at a billion tours per second it takes about two years
Explanation. $(n-1)!/2$ with $n=20$ is about $6\cdot10^{16}$: at $10^9$ tours per second it takes $6\cdot10^7$ seconds, almost two years. The length of a tour is computed in linear time.
oc-f-133 · level facile · notes
A symmetric TSP instance ($d_{ij}=d_{ji}$) can be seen as an asymmetric TSP (ATSP) instance. How are the tours of the two problems related?
  1. The ATSP tours are $n$ times more, one for every starting city
  2. The ATSP has fewer tours, because some arcs cannot be travelled
  3. Every symmetric tour corresponds to two ATSP tours, one per direction, of the same length
  4. Every symmetric tour corresponds to one ATSP tour only, because the distances coincide
Explanation. In the ATSP the two directions are different solutions; with symmetric distances they have the same length. This is why the tours are $(n-1)!$ in the ATSP and $(n-1)!/2$ in the symmetric TSP.
oc-f-277 · level facile · notes
In the travelling salesman family the $n$ cities have distances $d_{ij}$. What characterizes the metric TSP with respect to the TSP?
  1. The distances are all equal to $1$ or $2$
  2. The tour may visit a city several times
  3. The distances are symmetric and satisfy the triangle inequality $d_{ik}\le d_{ij}+d_{jk}$
  4. The distances may differ in the two directions
Explanation. The metric TSP is a special case of the symmetric TSP; in the ATSP, instead, $d_{ij}$ may differ from $d_{ji}$.
oc-f-278 · level facile · notes
In the TSP a solution is a tour, i.e., a permutation $\sigma$ of the $n$ cities read as a closed route. For $\sigma=(1,3,2,4)$, which sum is the length $L(\sigma)$?
  1. $d_{13}+d_{24}$
  2. $d_{13}+d_{32}+d_{24}+d_{41}$
  3. $d_{13}+d_{32}+d_{24}$
  4. $d_{12}+d_{23}+d_{34}+d_{41}$
Explanation. The tour goes back to the starting city: the $n$ distances between consecutive cities are summed, including the last one $d_{41}$.
oc-f-279 · level ipotesi · notes
In the TSP (symmetric distances) the distinct tours of $n$ cities are $(n-1)!/2$, and one assumes $n\ge3$. Why is the problem already trivial with $n=3$?
  1. There is a single tour: $(3-1)!/2=1$
  2. There are $3!=6$ different tours
  3. With $3$ cities the distances are not symmetric
  4. With $3$ cities no tour exists
Explanation. With three cities the tour $1\to2\to3\to1$ and its reverse are the same tour, and rotations do not count. With fewer than three cities there is not even a closed route of distinct cities.
oc-f-280 · level ipotesi · notes
In the TSP one assumes $d_{ij}=d_{ji}$, and the distinct tours of $n$ cities are $(n-1)!/2$. If the symmetry assumption is dropped (ATSP), how many distinct tours must be considered?
  1. Always $(n-1)!/2$
  2. $n!$, because rotations count too
  3. $2^n$, one per subset of arcs
  4. $(n-1)!$: a tour and its reverse in general have different lengths
Explanation. Without symmetry travelling the tour backwards changes its length, so the two directions are no longer identified; rotations remain the same tour.
oc-f-281 · level facile · notes
Three cities have symmetric distances $d_{12}=3$, $d_{23}=4$ and $d_{13}=9$. Do the distances satisfy the triangle inequality required by the metric TSP?
  1. Yes, because $9\le 3\cdot4$
  2. No, because $d_{12}\ne d_{23}$
  3. No: $d_{13}=9>d_{12}+d_{23}=7$
  4. Yes, because they are symmetric
Explanation. The inequality $d_{ik}\le d_{ij}+d_{jk}$ must be checked for every triple; here going through city $2$ is shorter than the direct link.
oc-f-282 · level facile · notes
Christofides (1976) gave an approximation algorithm for the metric TSP (symmetric distances with the triangle inequality). Which ratio does it guarantee?
  1. $1+\varepsilon$ for every $\varepsilon>0$
  2. $\frac32$
  3. $2$
  4. $\frac43$
Explanation. Christofides' algorithm finds a tour at most $\frac32$ times the optimum; the double tree guarantees $2$. For the general TSP no constant ratio is possible, if P $\ne$ NP.
oc-f-429 · level facile · notes
The travelling salesman family includes the TSP (symmetric distances $d_{ij}=d_{ji}$), the metric TSP (symmetric and with the triangle inequality) and the asymmetric TSP (ATSP, $d_{ij}$ may differ from $d_{ji}$). Which inclusion between the instances is correct?
  1. TSP $\subseteq$ metric $\subseteq$ ATSP
  2. metric $\subseteq$ ATSP $\subseteq$ TSP
  3. metric $\subseteq$ TSP $\subseteq$ ATSP
  4. ATSP $\subseteq$ TSP $\subseteq$ metric
Explanation. Every metric instance is symmetric, and every symmetric instance is an ATSP instance with $d_{ij}=d_{ji}$: the ATSP is the most general case.
oc-f-430 · level facile · notes
A TSP instance is given by an $n\times n$ matrix of distances $d_{ij}$, but the diagonal entries $d_{ii}$ are not used. Why?
  1. Because the tour returns to the start without covering any distance
  2. A tour visits every city once and never goes from a city to itself
  3. Because they are always $0$ and do not change the length
  4. Because by symmetry $d_{ii}=d_{ii}$ carries no information
Explanation. A tour is a permutation $\sigma$: the consecutive pairs $(\sigma_k,\sigma_{k+1})$ and $(\sigma_n,\sigma_1)$ are always distinct cities.
oc-f-431 · level facile · notes
In the symmetric TSP a tour is a permutation $\sigma$ of the $n$ cities read as a closed route. How many different permutations describe the same tour?
  1. $2n$: start and direction
  2. $n$: one per starting city
  3. $2$: one per travel direction
  4. $n!$: all the permutations
Explanation. One can start from any of the $n$ cities and, by symmetry, travel the tour in both directions: $n!/(2n)=(n-1)!/2$ distinct tours.
oc-f-432 · level facile · notes
TSP with $5$ cities and distances $d_{12}=5$, $d_{25}=4$, $d_{35}=7$, $d_{34}=4$, $d_{14}=2$ (symmetric). The length of $\sigma=(\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_n)$ is $L(\sigma)=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}d_{\sigma_k\sigma_{k+1}}+d_{\sigma_n\sigma_1}$. What is $L(1,2,5,3,4)$?
  1. $20$
  2. $18$
  3. $24$
  4. $22$
Explanation. $d_{12}+d_{25}+d_{53}+d_{34}+d_{41}=5+4+7+4+2=22$. $20$ forgets the return $d_{41}$ from city $4$ to city $1$.
oc-f-433 · level ipotesi · notes
In the TSP the distances are assumed symmetric, $d_{ij}=d_{ji}$. Under this assumption, how is an instance with $n$ cities represented, and how many numbers are needed?
  1. An $n\times n$ matrix in which all entries are used: $n^2$ numbers
  2. A path touching the cities in order: $n-1$ numbers
  3. A complete undirected graph with one edge $d_{ij}$ per pair: $n(n-1)/2$ numbers
  4. A complete directed graph with one arc per ordered pair: $n(n-1)$ numbers
Explanation. With $d_{ij}=d_{ji}$ one number per unordered pair is enough. In the ATSP, instead, $n(n-1)$ distances are needed, one per ordered pair.
oc-f-434 · level facile · notes
To enumerate the distinct tours of the symmetric TSP one fixes city $1$ as start ($(n-1)!$ sequences $(1,\sigma_2,\dots,\sigma_n)$). Which rule keeps a single tour for every tour/reverse-tour pair?
  1. Keep only the sequences with $\sigma_2=2$
  2. Keep only the sequences with $\sigma_2<\sigma_n$
  3. Keep only the sequences with $\sigma_2<\sigma_3$
  4. Keep only the sequences ending with city $n$
Explanation. The reverse of $(1,\sigma_2,\dots,\sigma_n)$ is $(1,\sigma_n,\dots,\sigma_2)$: it swaps the second and the last city. Exactly one of the two has $\sigma_2<\sigma_n$.
oc-f-435 · level facile · notes
In the symmetric TSP with $n=6$ cities the instance is a complete graph, and a tour is a cycle through all cities. How many edges does a tour use, and how many edges does the complete graph have?
  1. $6$ edges out of $15$
  2. $5$ edges out of $15$
  3. $6$ edges out of $30$
  4. $12$ edges out of $36$
Explanation. A cycle on $n$ vertices has $n$ edges, including the return one; the complete graph has $\binom62=15$. $5$ would be the edges of a path.
oc-f-436 · level facile · notes
In the TSP the nearest neighbour rule builds a tour by always moving to the closest unvisited city. In terms of the bounds of a minimization problem, what does the tour found provide?
  1. A dual bound: a lower bound $\LB\le z(I)$
  2. The optimal value $z(I)$, if there are no ties
  3. No bound: it only serves as a starting point
  4. A primal bound: an upper bound $\UB\ge z(I)$
Explanation. Every tour is a feasible solution, so its length is at least the optimal one. To know how far it is, a lower bound is needed.
oc-f-437 · level teorema · notes
In the symmetric TSP let $a_i\le b_i$ be the two smallest distances from city $i$ to the others; the bound is $\LB=\big\lceil\frac12\sum_i(a_i+b_i)\big\rceil\le z(I)$. When does the bound (without rounding) coincide with the optimal length?
  1. When the distances satisfy the triangle inequality
  2. Never: the bound is always strictly smaller than $z(I)$
  3. When a tour uses at every city its two shortest edges
  4. When all distances between the cities are distinct
Explanation. A tour uses two edges per city, of length at least $a_i+b_i$; the sum counts every edge twice. Equality holds if the tour uses the two minimum edges everywhere.
oc-f-438 · level facile · notes
Asymmetric TSP (ATSP) with $3$ cities: $d_{12}=1$, $d_{23}=2$, $d_{31}=3$, $d_{21}=5$, $d_{32}=4$, $d_{13}=6$ ($d_{ij}$ = distance from $i$ to $j$). How long are the tour $1\to2\to3\to1$ and its reverse $1\to3\to2\to1$?
  1. $6$ and $12$
  2. $6$ and $15$
  3. $6$ and $6$
  4. $15$ and $6$
Explanation. $d_{12}+d_{23}+d_{31}=1+2+3=6$; $d_{13}+d_{32}+d_{21}=6+4+5=15$. In the ATSP a tour and its reverse are different solutions.
oc-f-439 · level facile · notes
The TSP is strongly NP-hard. What does this imply, if $P\neq NP$?
  1. There is no pseudo-polynomial algorithm (polynomial in $n$ and in the distances)
  2. There is no exact algorithm at all for the TSP, not even a slow one
  3. There is a pseudo-polynomial algorithm, as for the knapsack
  4. Instances with more than $20$ cities cannot be solved to optimality
Explanation. A strongly NP-hard problem stays hard even with small numbers. Exact algorithms exist (brute force, branch-and-bound) and solve even huge instances.
oc-f-440 · level facile · notes
The TSP instance pla85900, with $85\,900$ points, was solved to optimality with the Concorde code. What does it show, given that the distinct tours are $(n-1)!/2$?
  1. With fast enough computers all tours can be enumerated
  2. Real instances have only a few feasible tours
  3. The TSP is not NP-hard on geometric instances of this kind
  4. Modern exact algorithms exclude almost all tours without enumerating them
Explanation. $85\,899!/2$ tours cannot be enumerated by any computer; bounds and cuts allow whole families of them to be discarded without examining them.
oc-f-441 · level facile · notes
The optimal tour of pla85900 (length $142\,382\,641$) was «certified» in 2009. What does certifying the optimality of a tour mean in a minimization problem?
  1. Checking that the tour visits every city once
  2. Showing that no exchange of two edges shortens the tour
  3. Exhibiting a lower bound equal to the tour length: zero gap
  4. Repeating the computation with a second code and getting the same tour
Explanation. A tour gives $\UB$; with an $\LB=\UB$ no tour can be shorter. The absence of improving moves gives only a local optimum.

7. Scheduling problems (30)

oc-f-025 · level facile · notes
In scheduling on identical machines $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ one assigns $n$ jobs to $m$ identical machines minimizing the maximum load; a schedule is a partition of the jobs into at most $m$ sets. How many distinct schedules are there with $n=4$ and $m=2$?
  1. $8$
  2. $16$
  3. $7$
  4. $24$
Explanation. There are $m^n=16$ assignments, but renaming the machines gives the same schedule: the partitions are $S(4,1)+S(4,2)=1+7=8$.
oc-f-026 · level teorema · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$, $n$ jobs of integer duration $t_j$ are assigned to $m$ identical machines minimizing the makespan (maximum load); let $z(I)$ be the optimal makespan. Which lower bound is valid for every instance?
  1. $z(I)\ge\max_j t_j+\left\lceil\sum_j t_j/m\right\rceil$
  2. $z(I)\ge\min_j t_j+\left\lceil\sum_j t_j/m\right\rceil$
  3. $z(I)\ge\sum_j t_j-\max_j t_j$
  4. $z(I)\ge\max\left\{\max_j t_j,\ \left\lceil\sum_j t_j/m\right\rceil\right\}$
Explanation. The machine of the longest job has load at least $\max_j t_j$, and the maximum load is at least the average $\sum_j t_j/m$, rounded up. The sums are not valid: with $m=2$ and $t=(1,1)$ we have $z(I)=1$; with $m=3$ and $t=(1,1,1)$ we have $z(I)=1<3-1$.
oc-f-027 · level facile · notes
In bin packing (BPP) the items go into bins of capacity $c$; in scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the jobs go onto $m$ identical machines. In what sense is one problem the «mirror» of the other?
  1. In both the number of bins (machines) is fixed and the maximum load is minimized; only the names of the data change
  2. In the BPP the number of bins is minimized; $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ is the case of the BPP with a single machine of capacity $c$
  3. In the BPP the capacity is fixed and the number of bins is minimized; in $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the number of machines is fixed and the maximum load is minimized
  4. In the BPP the number of bins is fixed and the maximum load is minimized; in $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the capacity is fixed and the number of machines is minimized
Explanation. Jobs and items, machines and bins correspond: in one problem the capacity is fixed, in the other the number of «bins», and the other quantity is optimized.
oc-f-117 · level facile · notes
In the three-field notation $\alpha\,|\,\beta\,|\,\gamma$ of parallel machine scheduling, the field $\alpha$ describes the machines: $P$, $Q$ or $R$. What does $Q\,||\,C_{\max}$ mean?
  1. Machines with different speeds $s_i$: job $j$ takes $t_j/s_i$ on machine $i$
  2. Identical machines: job $j$ takes $t_j$ on every machine
  3. Unrelated machines: job $j$ takes an arbitrary time $t_{ij}$ on machine $i$
  4. A fixed number of identical machines, not part of the input
Explanation. $P$ means identical machines, $Q$ uniform machines with speeds $s_i$ (times $t_j/s_i$), $R$ unrelated machines with arbitrary times $t_{ij}$; $C_{\max}$ in the third field is the makespan to minimize.
oc-f-118 · level facile · notes
In scheduling on identical machines $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ ($n$ jobs, $m$ machines), an assignment says on which machine every job goes. How many assignments are there with $n=5$ jobs and $m=3$ machines?
  1. $125$
  2. $15$
  3. $41$
  4. $243$
Explanation. Every job chooses one of the $3$ machines: $3^5=243$ assignments. Since the machines are identical, many assignments give the same schedule: the distinct schedules are $\sum_{k\le 3}S(5,k)=1+15+25=41$.
oc-f-119 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the machines are identical, so a schedule is a partition of the jobs into at most $m$ sets; $S(n,k)$ is the Stirling number of the second kind (partitions of $n$ elements into $k$ sets). How many distinct schedules are there with $n=5$ and $m=2$?
  1. $15$
  2. $31$
  3. $16$
  4. $32$
Explanation. $S(5,1)+S(5,2)=1+15=16$. The assignments are $2^5=32$; every schedule using two machines corresponds to two assignments, and so does the one using a single machine ($1+15$ partitions).
oc-f-120 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the load of a machine is the sum of the processing times of its jobs and the makespan is the largest load. Times $t=(5,3,4,2,6)$ on $m=3$ machines, with jobs $\{1,2\}$, $\{3,4\}$, $\{5\}$. What is the makespan?
  1. $6$
  2. $8$
  3. $20$
  4. $7$
Explanation. The loads are $5+3=8$, $4+2=6$ and $6$: the makespan is $8$. $20$ is the total load, $7=\lceil 20/3\rceil$ is the average lower bound, not the makespan of this schedule.
oc-f-121 · level teorema · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ ($m=2$ identical machines) the processing times are $t=(4,4,3,3,2)$. The schedule $\{4,4\}$, $\{3,3,2\}$ has makespan $8$. What can be concluded with the lower bound $\max\{\max_j t_j,\lceil\sum_j t_j/m\rceil\}$?
  1. The bound is $8$, hence the schedule is optimal
  2. The bound is $4$, hence the schedule can be improved
  3. The bound is $8$, but a bound never proves optimality
  4. The bound is $16$, hence the schedule is infeasible
Explanation. $\sum_j t_j=16$ and $\lceil 16/2\rceil=8\ge\max_j t_j=4$: the bound is $8$. A schedule whose makespan equals a lower bound is optimal.
oc-f-122 · level facile · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=2$ machines, every job is assigned, in the given order, to the least loaded machine (ties: the one with smaller index). Processing times in order $t=(3,5,2,4,6)$. Which makespan is obtained?
  1. $10$
  2. $9$
  3. $12$
  4. $11$
Explanation. Loads: $(3,0)\to(3,5)\to(5,5)\to(9,5)\to(9,11)$: makespan $11$. The schedule $\{6,4\}$, $\{5,3,2\}$ has makespan $10=\lceil 20/2\rceil$, so the rule does not give the optimum.
oc-f-123 · level facile · notes
Bin packing (BPP) asks for the minimum number of bins of capacity $C$; scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ asks for the minimum makespan with $m$ machines. How does the question «is there a schedule with makespan at most $C$?» read in bin packing terms?
  1. Is the weight bound $\lceil\sum_j t_j/C\rceil$ at most $C$?
  2. Is there a bin of capacity $C$ containing all the items?
  3. Do the items of weight $t_j$ fit into $m$ bins of capacity $C$?
  4. Do the items of weight $t_j$ fit into $C$ bins of capacity $m$?
Explanation. The machines are the bins, the makespan is the capacity: a schedule with makespan at most $C$ exists if and only if the jobs fit into $m$ bins of capacity $C$. In the BPP the capacity is fixed, in $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the number of bins.
oc-f-124 · level teorema · notes
In scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$, the lower bound $\mathrm{LB}=\max\{\max_j t_j,\lceil\sum_j t_j/m\rceil\}$ is always valid. With $m=2$ machines and three jobs of processing time $t=(2,2,2)$, what are $\mathrm{LB}$ and the optimal makespan $z(I)$?
  1. $\mathrm{LB}=4$ and $z(I)=4$: the bound rounds up to a multiple of $t_j$
  2. $\mathrm{LB}=3$ and $z(I)=4$: the bound is not always attained
  3. $\mathrm{LB}=3$ and $z(I)=3$: the bound is always attained
  4. $\mathrm{LB}=2$ and $z(I)=4$: the bound is the longest job
Explanation. $\lceil 6/2\rceil=3>2$, hence $\mathrm{LB}=3$; but jobs cannot be split, and one machine gets at least two jobs: $z(I)=4$. The bound is valid but not always attained.
oc-f-125 · level facile · notes
In parallel machine scheduling, what distinguishes $R\,||\,C_{\max}$ (unrelated machines) from $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ (identical machines)?
  1. In $R\,||\,C_{\max}$ the time $t_{ij}$ of job $j$ depends on machine $i$ arbitrarily
  2. In $R\,||\,C_{\max}$ the machines have speeds $s_i$ and job $j$ takes $t_j/s_i$
  3. In $R\,||\,C_{\max}$ the number of machines is fixed and not part of the input
  4. In $R\,||\,C_{\max}$ jobs can be interrupted and resumed on another machine
Explanation. $R$ means unrelated machines, with arbitrary times $t_{ij}$; the speeds $s_i$ are the case $Q$, and $P$ is the case $t_{ij}=t_j$ for every machine.
oc-f-283 · level facile · notes
In the three-field notation of parallel machine scheduling, what distinguishes $R\,||\,C_{\max}$ from $P\,||\,C_{\max}$?
  1. In $R$ the sum of the completion times is minimized
  2. In $R$ every job can be split among several machines
  3. In $R$ job $j$ requires a time $t_{jh}$ depending on the machine $h$; in $P$ it requires $t_j$ on every machine
  4. In $R$ the machines have different but proportional speeds
Explanation. $P$: identical machines; $Q$: machines with different speeds; $R$: unrelated times, arbitrary for every job-machine pair. The third field $C_{\max}$ is the same.
oc-f-284 · level ipotesi · notes
In $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ ($n$ jobs of duration $t_j$ on $m$ identical machines, the makespan is minimized) one assumes $n>m$. What happens if $n\le m$?
  1. The optimal makespan is $\sum_j t_j$
  2. Every job has a machine of its own and the optimal makespan is $\max_j t_j$
  3. The instance is infeasible
  4. The optimal makespan is $\sum_j t_j/m$
Explanation. With enough machines no job waits, and no solution does better than the longest job: the problem is trivial.
oc-f-285 · level facile · notes
Graham proved that greedy scheduling of any list of jobs on $m$ identical machines gives a makespan at most $2-\frac1m$ times the optimum, and the list sorted by non-increasing duration (LPT) at most $\frac43-\frac1{3m}$ times. What are the two ratios for $m=3$?
  1. $\frac53$ and $\frac{11}9$
  2. $\frac53$ and $\frac43$
  3. $2$ and $\frac43$
  4. $\frac32$ and $\frac{11}9$
Explanation. $2-\frac13=\frac53$ and $\frac43-\frac19=\frac{11}9$. The ratios without the term in $m$ are the limits for $m\to\infty$.
oc-f-442 · level facile · notes
In parallel machine scheduling the makespan $C_{\max}=\max_h L_h$ is minimized, where the load $L_h$ is the sum of the times of the jobs of machine $h$. Why does minimizing $C_{\max}$ push towards balancing the loads?
  1. Because every machine must receive the same number of jobs
  2. Because the makespan coincides with the average load of the machines
  3. Because with identical machines the loads are always all equal
  4. The sum of the loads is fixed: the maximum is low only if they are close
Explanation. Every schedule has $\sum_h L_h=\sum_j t_j$, hence $\max_h L_h\ge\sum_j t_j/m$, with equality only for equal loads. The number of jobs per machine does not matter.
oc-f-443 · level facile · notes
In $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ the jobs assigned to a machine are processed one after the other, without idle time. Does the order of the jobs on the same machine change the makespan?
  1. Yes: it pays to process the shortest jobs first
  2. It depends on the number of available machines $m$
  3. No: the load is the sum of the times, in any order
  4. Yes: it pays to process the longest jobs first
Explanation. A solution is just an assignment: machine $h$ finishes at time $L_h=\sum_{j\in J_h}t_j$. The order matters for other objectives, such as $\sum_j C_j$.
oc-f-444 · level facile · notes
In the three-field notation $\alpha\,|\,\beta\,|\,\gamma$ of scheduling ($\alpha$ = machines, $\beta$ = job characteristics, $\gamma$ = objective), which problem does $1\,|\,prec\,|\,\sum_j C_j$ denote?
  1. A single machine, no constraints, sum of tardiness
  2. A single machine, precedence constraints, sum of completion times
  3. One machine per job, precedence constraints, makespan
  4. Identical machines, release dates, sum of completion times
Explanation. $\alpha=1$: one machine; $\beta=prec$: precedences among the jobs; $\gamma=\sum_jC_j$: sum of completion times. The makespan would be $C_{\max}$.
oc-f-445 · level facile · notes
$P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=3$ machines and times $t=(5,5,4,4,3,3,3)$. Which makespan does the assignment $\mathrm{mach}=(1,2,3,1,2,3,3)$ ($\mathrm{mach}_j$ = machine of job $j$) have?
  1. $10$
  2. $9$
  3. $8$
  4. $27$
Explanation. $J_1=\{1,4\}$: $9$; $J_2=\{2,5\}$: $8$; $J_3=\{3,6,7\}$: $4+3+3=10$. The makespan is the maximum load, $10$; $9=\lceil27/3\rceil$ is the lower bound.
oc-f-446 · level ipotesi · notes
In $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ one assumes $n>m$. In the limit case $n=m+1$ ($m=3$ machines, times $t=(7,6,2,3)$), what is the optimal makespan?
  1. $13$: the two longest jobs on the same machine
  2. $9$: the longest job together with a short one
  3. $6$: the average $\sum_j t_j/m$ of the loads
  4. $7$: the two shortest jobs together
Explanation. With $m+1$ jobs one machine gets two of them: it pays to pair the shortest ones ($2+3=5$); the makespan is $\max\{7,6,5\}=7$, equal to the lower bound $\max_j t_j$.
oc-f-447 · level ipotesi · notes
In $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ ($n$ jobs, $m$ identical machines) one assumes $n>m$. Why, under this assumption, does at least one machine process at least two jobs in every schedule?
  1. Because every machine must receive at least one job
  2. It is not true: a machine may stay empty
  3. By the pigeonhole principle: $n>m$ jobs on $m$ machines
  4. Because the optimal makespan always exceeds $\max_j t_j$
Explanation. If every machine had at most one job, there would be at most $m<n$ jobs. The optimal makespan may still equal $\max_j t_j$.
oc-f-448 · level teorema · notes
In $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=3$ identical machines, two optimal schedules (partitions of the jobs into $3$ non-empty sets) correspond to $12$ of the $3^n$ assignments $\mathrm{mach}$ (job $\to$ machine). Why $12$?
  1. Every schedule gives $6$ assignments, one per order of the jobs
  2. Every schedule with $3$ non-empty machines gives $3!=6$ assignments, renaming the machines
  3. Every schedule gives $3$ assignments, one per most loaded machine
  4. Every schedule gives $2^3$ assignments, one per subset of machines
Explanation. The $3!$ permutations of the machine labels give different assignments of the same schedule: $2\cdot6=12$.
oc-f-449 · level facile · notes
In $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ ($m=3$, times $t=(5,5,4,4,3,3,3)$) every job, in the given order, is assigned to the least loaded machine, obtaining makespan $11$. What does this first schedule by hand show?
  1. An upper bound, without knowing how far from optimal it is
  2. It proves that the optimal makespan is exactly $11$
  3. It shows that the optimal makespan is at least $11$
  4. It shows that the least loaded machine is always the optimal choice
Explanation. A feasible schedule bounds the optimum from above ($z(I)\le11$). The lower bound $\max\{5,\lceil27/3\rceil\}=9$ leaves a gap, and the optimal schedule has makespan $9$.
oc-f-450 · level teorema · notes
In $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ (integer times $t_j$, $m$ machines) the lower bound contains $\lceil\sum_j t_j/m\rceil$ instead of the average $\sum_j t_j/m$. Why can it be rounded up?
  1. Because the average of the loads is always an integer
  2. Because every machine always receives at least one job
  3. Because $\max_j t_j$ is an integer number as well
  4. The maximum load is at least the average and is an integer
Explanation. The makespan is a sum of integer times, hence an integer, and an integer not smaller than a real number $x$ is not smaller than $\lceil x\rceil$.
oc-f-451 · level facile · notes
Bin packing (BPP) and scheduling $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ put items/jobs into bins/machines. Which quantities do they fix and which do they minimize?
  1. Both fix the capacity and minimize the number of bins or machines
  2. Both fix the number of bins or machines and minimize the load
  3. BPP: fixes capacity, minimizes bins; $P\,||\,C_{\max}$: fixes machines, minimizes the maximum load
  4. BPP: fixes the bins and minimizes the capacity; $P\,||\,C_{\max}$: fixes the load and minimizes the machines
Explanation. The two problems swap what is given and what is optimized: the machines are bins of capacity $T$, and $T$ is the makespan to be minimized.
oc-f-452 · level facile · notes
In $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ there is a schedule with makespan at most $T$ if and only if the jobs (items of weight $t_j$) fit into $m$ bins of capacity $T$. With $m=2$ and $t=(6,5,4,3,2)$, what is the minimum $T$ for which they fit, i.e., the optimal makespan?
  1. $6$
  2. $10$
  3. $11$
  4. $20$
Explanation. $\lceil20/2\rceil=10$ is a lower bound, and $\{6,4\}$, $\{5,3,2\}$ fill two bins of capacity $10$: the minimum is $10$.
oc-f-453 · level teorema · notes
In $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ with $m=3$ machines, it is known that the jobs, as items of weight $t_j$, need at least $4$ bins of capacity $12$ in bin packing. What follows for the optimal makespan $z(I)$?
  1. No schedule has makespan $\le12$, so $z(I)\ge13$
  2. $z(I)=12$, because the capacity equals the makespan
  3. $z(I)\le12$, because $4$ bins suffice
  4. $z(I)\ge16=4\cdot12/3$
Explanation. Makespan $\le12$ on $3$ machines is equivalent to fitting into $3$ bins of capacity $12$, impossible if $4$ are needed. With integer times $z(I)\ge13$ follows.
oc-f-454 · level facile · notes
Graham (1966): processing any list of jobs greedily (every job to the least loaded machine) on $m$ identical machines, the makespan is at most $(2-\frac1m)$ times the optimum. With $m=3$ and optimal makespan $9$, how large can the greedy makespan be at most?
  1. $18$
  2. $12$
  3. $13$
  4. $15$
Explanation. $(2-\frac13)\cdot9=\frac53\cdot9=15$. $18$ uses ratio $2$; $12=\frac43\cdot9$ confuses it with the guarantee of the LPT rule.
oc-f-455 · level facile · notes
Graham (1969): the LPT rule (list sorted by non-increasing processing time, every job to the least loaded machine) has ratio $\frac43-\frac1{3m}$ on $m$ identical machines. What is the ratio with $m=2$?
  1. $\frac43$
  2. $\frac{11}{9}$
  3. $\frac76$
  4. $\frac32$
Explanation. $\frac43-\frac16=\frac86-\frac16=\frac76$. $\frac32=2-\frac12$ is the ratio of an arbitrary list with $m=2$; $\frac{11}9$ is LPT with $m=3$.
oc-f-456 · level teorema · notes
For $P\,||\,C_{\max}$ greedy on an arbitrary list has ratio $2-\frac1m$ and the LPT rule $\frac43-\frac1{3m}$. What happens to the two ratios when the number $m$ of machines grows?
  1. They stay constant, since they do not depend on $m$
  2. They grow towards $2$ and towards $\frac43$
  3. Both decrease towards $1$
  4. Both grow towards $2$
Explanation. The subtracted terms $\frac1m$ and $\frac1{3m}$ tend to $0$: the guarantees get worse with $m$, but stay below $2$ and below $\frac43$.

8. Lot sizing problems (43)

oc-f-028 · level facile · notes
Capacitated lot sizing (CLS): $5$ periods, production capacities $cap=(40,30,30,20,30)$, total demand $D=110$, setup costs $sc=(50,60,40,70,55)$ ($sc_t$ is paid if production occurs in period $t$). The combinatorial lower bound sums the $k$ smallest setup costs, where $k$ is the minimum number of periods whose capacities cover $D$. What is its value?
  1. $235$
  2. $275$
  3. $205$
  4. $145$
Explanation. The largest capacities give $40+30+30=100<110$ and $40+30+30+30=130\ge 110$: at least $k=4$ setups are needed. The $4$ smallest setup costs sum to $40+50+55+60=205$.
oc-f-029 · level facile · notes
In capacitated lot sizing (CLS) every period $t\in T$ has demand $d_t$, production capacity $cap_t$, setup cost $sc_t$ and final inventory $s_t$. What is uncapacitated lot sizing (ULS)?
  1. The CLS with a single period, i.e., $T=\{1\}$ and capacity $cap_1\ge d_1$
  2. The CLS in which the capacities are not binding, e.g., $cap_t\ge\sum_{u\in T}d_u$ for every $t$
  3. The CLS in which setups are free, i.e., $sc_t=0$ for every period $t\in T$
  4. The CLS in which inventory is forbidden, i.e., $s_t=0$ for every period $t\in T$
Explanation. In the ULS every period can produce as much as it wants; setup, variable and holding costs remain.
oc-f-030 · level facile · notes
In lot sizing (demand $d_t$, production $x_t$ and final inventory $s_t$ in every period $t$; the setup is paid when $x_t>0$), which statement is correct?
  1. Without capacities some optimal plan produces only with zero incoming inventory; with capacities it may not exist
  2. With or without capacities some optimal plan produces only with zero incoming inventory
  3. Without capacities every optimal plan produces in all periods with positive demand; with capacities not
  4. With or without capacities the production of a period can cover the demand of an earlier period
Explanation. The Wagner-Whitin property (zero-inventory ordering) holds without capacities; with capacities there are instances in which every optimal plan produces with positive inventory. Production never covers past demand.
oc-f-108 · level ipotesi · notes
In capacitated lot sizing (CLS: periods $t$ with demand $d_t$, capacity $cap_t$, setup cost $sc_t$ paid if we produce in $t$; zero initial stock), let $D=\sum_t d_t$. Why does every feasible plan have at least $k$ setups, with $k$ the minimum number of periods whose capacities cover $D$?
  1. Setup costs are positive, so an optimal plan uses the $k$ cheapest setup periods
  2. The final stock must be zero, so we produce exactly $d_t$ in every period with a setup
  3. We produce only in periods with a setup, at most $cap_t$ in each, and the total production must cover $D$
  4. Every period with positive demand needs a setup, and the periods with demand are at least $k$
Explanation. Without initial stock the total production is at least $D$, and the setup periods produce at most the sum of their capacities; hence at least $k$ setup periods are needed. The bound then adds the $k$ smallest setup costs.
oc-f-109 · level teorema · notes
In capacitated lot sizing (CLS), the combinatorial lower bound adds the $k$ smallest setup costs, where $k$ is the minimum number of periods whose capacities $cap_t$ cover the total demand $D$. How is $k$ computed?
  1. As $\lceil D/\min_t cap_t\rceil$, the number of periods with the minimum capacity needed
  2. Sorting the capacities in non-increasing order: $k$ is the smallest number of largest capacities summing to at least $D$
  3. Sorting the capacities in non-decreasing order: $k$ is the smallest number of smallest capacities summing to at least $D$
  4. Sorting the setup costs in non-decreasing order and adding the capacities of those periods until they cover $D$
Explanation. To get the minimum number of periods one takes the largest capacities. The bound is valid because every plan has at least $k$ setups, whose cost is at least the sum of the $k$ smallest costs, even if in different periods.
oc-f-110 · level facile · notes
In capacitated lot sizing (CLS) over $m$ periods, the combinatorial part of the decision is the choice of the setup periods (those in which we produce). With $m=6$ periods, how many choices are there, before checking the capacities?
  1. $64$
  2. $720$
  3. $36$
  4. $63$
Explanation. Every period has a setup or not: $2^6=64$ subsets. Only some of them are feasible, and each can carry many different production plans.
oc-f-111 · level facile · notes
Lot sizing over $3$ periods: demands $d=(3,2,4)$, setup cost $50$ in every period with production, cost $10$ per unit of stock at the end of a period, zero variable cost, non-binding capacities. What is the cost of the plan $x=(5,0,4)$, which produces in period $1$ also the demand of period $2$?
  1. $150$
  2. $100$
  3. $140$
  4. $120$
Explanation. Two setups ($100$) and a stock of $2$ units at the end of period $1$ ($20$): total $120$. The lot-for-lot plan $(3,2,4)$ costs $150$, with three setups and no stock.
oc-f-112 · level facile · notes
In capacitated lot sizing (CLS) the stock at the end of a period is $s_t=\sum_{u\le t}(x_u-d_u)$ (zero initial stock) and must be non-negative. With demands $d=(3,2,4)$ and capacity $5$ in every period, which plan $x$ is feasible?
  1. $x=(6,0,3)$
  2. $x=(3,2,3)$
  3. $x=(3,4,2)$
  4. $x=(2,3,4)$
Explanation. $(3,4,2)$ gives stocks $(0,2,0)$. $(2,3,4)$ has $s_1=-1$ (it produces late), $(6,0,3)$ exceeds the capacity $5$, $(3,2,3)$ leaves one unit of period $3$ uncovered.
oc-f-113 · level facile · notes
